```typescript
function highlightHTML(code: string): string {
  let escaped = code
    .replace(/&/g, "&amp;")
    .replace(/</g, "&lt;")
    .replace(/>/g, "&gt;");
  
  // Highlight comments
  escaped = escaped.replace(/(&lt;!--[\s\S]*?--&gt;)/g, '<span class="text-slate-500 font-normal italic">$1</span>');
  
  // Highlight tags: &lt;tag or &lt;/tag
  escaped = escaped.replace(/(&lt;\/?[a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)/g, '<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
  
  // Highlight tag ends: &gt; or /&gt;
  escaped = escaped.replace(/(\/?&gt;)/g, '<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
  
  // Highlight attribute values: ="value"
  escaped = escaped.replace(/(=["'][^"']*?["'])/g, '<span class="text-amber-300">$1</span>');
  
  // Highlight attributes: attributeName=
  escaped = escaped.replace(/(\s[a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)=/g, ' <span class="text-cyan-400">$1</span>=');
  
  return escaped;
}
```
Wait! Look at this replacement:
`escaped = escaped.replace(/(\s[a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)=/g, ' <span class="text-cyan-400">$1</span>=');`
And:
`escaped = escaped.replace(/(=["'][^"']*?["'])/g, '<span class="text-amber-300">$1</span>');`
Wait! `class="text-blue-400 font-bold"` is inserted by tag highlight.
Then, attribute values like `="text-blue-400 font-bold"` get highlighted *again* by attributes or string match regex!
For example:
- `="text-blue-400 font-bold"` contains `text-blue-400 font-bold`.
- The string replace `/(=["'][^"']*?["'])/g` will match `="text-blue-400 font-bold"` and wrap it in `<span class="text-amber-300">...</span>`.
- This creates nested span tags like `<span class="text-amber-300">="text-blue-400 font-bold"</span>`.
- More catastrophically, the tag ends and tag starts get wrapped again and again because the regex matches are run sequentially on the *entire string* including the already injected `<span class="...">` tags!
Yes! The injected HTML markup tags themselves are matched by subsequent regex replacements!
For example, `class=` in `<span class="text-slate-500">` will match `(\s[a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)=` and get highlighted as an attribute!
So `<span class="text-slate-500">` becomes `<span <span class="text-cyan-400">class</span>="text-slate-500">`!
This is a classic syntax highlight collision bug that corrupts the HTML code inside the pre element completely, resulting in garbled text display in the playground!

Let's think: how can we write a robust token-based highlighter, or do highlighting *without* replacing characters that are part of injected tags?
Or, how does W3Schools or other online editors handle this?
They use standard textareas with solid colors, or they use custom code editors like Monaco or CodeMirror, or they write a clean tokenizer.
Wait, can we write a simple tokenizer that parses the string into tokens *first*, highlights the tokens, and then joins them?
Yes! A tokenizer is extremely clean and 100% robust against regex collision bugs!
Let's write a simple tokenizer for HTML, CSS, and JS:
Wait! Since this is a lightweight simulation playground, we don't need a full compiler parser, just a simple tokenizer that splits the code into safe segments.
Let's see: how can we tokenize HTML?
An HTML string can be split by tags and text:
- Comments: `<!-- ... -->`
- Tags: `<...>`
- Text: anything else.
If we split by:
`const parts = code.split(/(<!--[\s\S]*?-->|<[^>]+>)/);`
Then for each part:
- If it starts with `<!--` and ends with `-->`, it's a comment. We escape it and wrap it in a comment span.
- If it starts with `<` and ends with `>`, it's an HTML tag! Inside the tag:
  - We can parse the tag name, attributes, and attribute values.
  - Wait, to prevent nested highlighting, we can parse the tag parts using a localized regex *before* wrapping the tag itself.
  - Or even simpler: we escape the tag characters (`<` to `&lt;` and `>` to `&gt;`), highlight attributes/values within that isolated tag string, and then wrap the result. Because we do it on each tag string *in isolation* before joining, it will never match the outer injected HTML tags!
- If it's text: we just escape it (`&` to `&amp;`, `<` to `&lt;`, `>` to `&gt;`) and leave it as is.
This is brilliant and extremely robust! It prevents any self-nested highlighting because the markup tags are injected *after* all tag-level regex matching has completed!

Let's write this tag-based tokenizer for HTML:
```typescript
function highlightHTML(code: string): string {
  // Split by comments and tags
  const parts = code.split(/(<!--[\s\S]*?-->|<[^>]+>)/g);
  return parts.map(part => {
    if (!part) return "";
    
    // Comment
    if (part.startsWith("<!--") && part.endsWith("-->")) {
      const escaped = escapeHtml(part);
      return `<span class="text-slate-500 font-normal italic">${escaped}</span>`;
    }
    
    // Tag
    if (part.startsWith("<") && part.endsWith(">")) {
      // Is it a closing tag?
      const isClosing = part.startsWith("</");
      const cleanTag = part.replace(/[<>]/g, ""); // strip < and >
      
      // Highlight attributes inside the clean tag
      // Attribute values: ="..." or ='...'
      let highlighted = cleanTag.replace(/(=["'][^"']*?["'])/g, '<span class="text-amber-300">$1</span>');
      
      // Attributes: name=
      highlighted = highlighted.replace(/(\s[a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)=/g, ' <span class="text-cyan-400">$1</span>=');
      
      // Tag name: at the start of the tag
      if (isClosing) {
        // e.g. /div
        highlighted = highlighted.replace(/^(\/[a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)/, '<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
      } else {
        // e.g. div or div class="foo"
        highlighted = highlighted.replace(/^([a-zA-Z0-9\-]+)/, '<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
      }
      
      return `<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">&lt;</span>${highlighted}<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">&gt;</span>`;
    }
    
    // Text content: just escape it
    return escapeHtml(part);
  }).join("");
}

function escapeHtml(text: string): string {
  return text
    .replace(/&/g, "&amp;")
    .replace(/</g, "&lt;")
    .replace(/>/g, "&gt;");
}
```

Wait, this is absolutely beautiful and completely resolves any collision or nesting issues in HTML!

Now let's design a similar tokenizer for CSS:
For CSS, we can split the string by comments, blocks, and properties:
Wait, a simpler way is to highlight using a token regex. What are the elements in CSS?
- Comments: `/* ... */`
- Selectors: e.g. `body`, `.class`, `#id`
- Rules: `{ ... }`
Let's see: we can split by comments:
`const parts = code.split(/(\/\*[\s\S]*?\*\/)/g);`
For each part:
- If it's a comment: wrap it in comment span.
- Otherwise, it's regular CSS. Inside regular CSS, we can split by blocks using `{` and `}`:
  `const subParts = part.split(/([{}])/g);`
  For each subPart:
  - If it is `{` or `}`: wrap in bracket span.
  - If it is followed by a `{` (meaning it's a selector): wrap in selector span.
  Wait! Let's think: how do we know if a subPart is a selector or a rule body?
  In a list of subParts split by `{` and `}`:
  - If we keep track of whether we are inside a rule body (state variable `inRule = false`):
    - When we encounter `{`, we set `inRule = true`.
    - When we encounter `}`, we set `inRule = false`.
    - If `inRule` is false and the subPart is not `{` or `}`, it is a selector! We can escape it and wrap it in selector span.
    - If `inRule` is true and the subPart is not `{` or `}`, it is a rule body (properties and values):
      Inside the rule body, we can split by declarations (separated by `;`):
      - For each declaration, we can split by `:`:
        - The part before `:` is the property. We escape it and wrap in property span.
        - The part after `:` is the value. We escape it and wrap in value span.
This stateful parser is 100% robust and extremely clean! It will never mismatch properties or selectors!

Let's write this parser for CSS:
```typescript
function highlightCSS(code: string): string {
  const parts = code.split(/(\/\*[\s\S]*?\*\/)/g);
  let inRule = false;
  
  return parts.map(part => {
    if (!part) return "";
    
    // Comment
    if (part.startsWith("/*") && part.endsWith("*/")) {
      return `<span class="text-slate-500 italic">${escapeHtml(part)}</span>`;
    }
    
    // Split by brackets
    const subParts = part.split(/([{}])/g);
    return subParts.map(sub => {
      if (sub === "{") {
        inRule = true;
        return '<span class="text-yellow-500 font-bold">{</span>';
      }
      if (sub === "}") {
        inRule = false;
        return '<span class="text-yellow-500 font-bold">}</span>';
      }
      if (!sub.trim()) return sub;
      
      if (!inRule) {
        // It's a selector
        return `<span class="text-orange-400 font-bold">${escapeHtml(sub)}</span>`;
      } else {
        // It's a rule body (declarations)
        const decls = sub.split(/(;)/g);
        return decls.map(decl => {
          if (decl === ";") {
            return '<span class="text-slate-400">;</span>';
          }
          if (!decl.trim()) return decl;
          
          const colonIdx = decl.indexOf(":");
          if (colonIdx >= 0) {
            const prop = decl.slice(0, colonIdx);
            const val = decl.slice(colonIdx + 1);
            return `<span class="text-cyan-400">${escapeHtml(prop)}</span>:<span class="text-emerald-400">${escapeHtml(val)}</span>`;
          }
          return escapeHtml(decl);
        }).join("");
      }
    }).join("");
  }).join("");
}
```
Oh my goodness! This is incredibly simple, 100% correct, and works flawlessly without any regex replacement conflicts!

Now let's design a similar robust highlighter/tokenizer for JavaScript:
For JavaScript:
- Strings: `"..."`, `'...'`, `` `...` ``
- Comments: `// ...` or `/* ... */`
- Keywords
- Numbers
Let's see: we can split by strings and comments first!
The regex to match strings and comments in JS is:
`const tokens = code.split(/(\/\/[^\n]*|\/\*[\s\S]*?\*\/|"[^"\\]*(?:\\.[^"\\]*)*"|'[^'\\]*(?:\\.[^'\\]*)*'|`[^`\\]*(?:\\.[`\\]*)*`)/g);`
Let's check this regex:
- `\/\/[^\n]*` matches single line comments.
- `\/\*[\s\S]*?\*\/` matches multi-line comments.
- `"[^"\\]*(?:\\.[^"\\]*)*"` matches double quoted strings.
- `'[^'\\]*(?:\\.[^'\\]*)*'` matches single quoted strings.
- `` `[^`\\]*(?:\\.[`\\]*)*` `` matches backtick strings.
This regex is extremely standard and successfully extracts comments and strings in isolation!
For each token:
- If it starts with `//` or `/*`, it's a comment. We escape it and wrap in comment span.
- If it starts with `"`, `'`, or `` ` ``, it's a string. We escape it and wrap in string span.
- Otherwise, it's normal code (keywords, variables, symbols). Inside this code:
  - We escape the characters first: `const escaped = escapeHtml(token);`
  - Then we highlight keywords:
    We want to match keywords like `const`, `function`, etc. Since we escaped first, we can replace keywords with word boundaries `\b`:
    Wait, can we use a regex for keywords?
    Yes, because there are no HTML tags in `escaped` yet (we haven't injected any spans in this token yet)! So it is 100% safe to do regex replacements on keywords and numbers inside this isolated, escaped code token!
    Let's write this:
    ```typescript
    const keywords = [
      "const", "let", "var", "function", "return", "if", "else", "for", "while", 
      "do", "switch", "case", "break", "continue", "new", "this", "class", "export", 
      "import", "from", "default", "true", "false", "null", "undefined", "document", "window"
    ];
    let highlighted = escaped;
    // Highlight numbers
    highlighted = highlighted.replace(/\b(\d+)\b/g, '<span class="text-purple-400">$1</span>');
    
    // Highlight keywords
    keywords.forEach(kw => {
      const regex = new RegExp(`\\b(${kw})\\b`, "g");
      highlighted = highlighted.replace(regex, '<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
    });
    
    // Highlight methods/functions: console.log or addEventListener
    highlighted = highlighted.replace(/(\.[a-zA-Z0-9_]+)(?=\()/g, '.<span class="text-yellow-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
    ```
    Wait! Is this correct?
    Yes! Because this token contains NO HTML tags, doing regex on it will NEVER conflict with any injected spans!
This is absolutely perfect and completely solves the JavaScript syntax highlighter bugs!

Let's write `highlightJS`:
```typescript
function highlightJS(code: string): string {
  const parts = code.split(/(\/\/[^\n]*|\/\*[\s\S]*?\*\/|"[^"\\]*(?:\\.[^"\\]*)*"|'[^'\\]*(?:\\.[^'\\]*)*'|`[^`\\]*(?:\\.[`\\]*)*`)/g);
  const keywords = [
    "const", "let", "var", "function", "return", "if", "else", "for", "while", 
    "do", "switch", "case", "break", "continue", "new", "this", "class", "export", 
    "import", "from", "default", "true", "false", "null", "undefined", "document", "window"
  ];
  
  return parts.map(part => {
    if (!part) return "";
    
    // Comments
    if (part.startsWith("//") || part.startsWith("/*")) {
      return `<span class="text-slate-500 italic">${escapeHtml(part)}</span>`;
    }
    
    // Strings
    if (part.startsWith('"') || part.startsWith("'") || part.startsWith("`")) {
      return `<span class="text-amber-300">${escapeHtml(part)}</span>`;
    }
    
    // Normal code
    const escaped = escapeHtml(part);
    let highlighted = escaped;
    
    // Highlight numbers
    highlighted = highlighted.replace(/\b(\d+)\b/g, '<span class="text-purple-400">$1</span>');
    
    // Highlight keywords
    keywords.forEach(kw => {
      const regex = new RegExp(`\\b(${kw})\\b`, "g");
      highlighted = highlighted.replace(regex, '<span class="text-blue-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
    });
    
    // Highlight methods
    highlighted = highlighted.replace(/(\.[a-zA-Z0-9_]+)(?=\()/g, '.<span class="text-yellow-400 font-bold">$1</span>');
    
    return highlighted;
  }).join("");
}
```
This is absolutely gorgeous and works perfectly!

   to BOTH the `pre` and `textarea` tags, they will be perfectly matched in every single browser!
